(2) Prisma's failed-migration marker is DB-side: a mid-apply failure leaves a `_prisma_migrations` row with finished_at NULL, and every later `prisma migrate deploy` — including from the rolled-back :previous-api image, which doesn't even contain the new migration file — exits with P3009 until a human runs `prisma migrate resolve --rolled-back <name>` or restores the pre-deploy snapshot. (3) The auto-rollback step is image-and-Caddyfile only, fully DB-unaware; its own MIGRATION SAFETY NOTE covers only the applied-then-crash case, not failed-mid-apply. (4) Compounding: the rollback script runs `set -euo pipefail` and `app` depends_on `api: service_healthy`, so the rollback's `docker compose up -d` aborts on "dependency failed to start" and the Caddyfile restore after it never executes. (5) No P3009/`migrate resolve` runbook exists anywhere in the repo (grep confirms zero hits). (6) CI smoke boots against a fresh DB, so data-dependent failures (the realistic class: new unique/NOT NULL constraint vs real pilot rows) pass CI and fail only on the VPS. Not by_design: the compose comment at docker-compose.yml:403-405 explicitly claims auto-rollback covers deploy-time migration failure — the design's own assumption is defeated for this class. Severity correctly medium: concrete impact is a total api outage (webhook ingest, sockets, REST all down) that the safety net cannot recover, requiring SSH + manual resolve/restore — but it is gated on a migration actually failing mid-apply in production, an edge operational event rather than normal pilot use, and a manual recovery path (pre-deploy snapshot) already exists, just undocumented for this specific marker.

### 39. Rollback chain can promote a never-shipped image: :previous is retagged from registry :latest before knowing it was ever deployed (Trivy/smoke-blocked images poison the rollback target)

**Severity:** medium · **Location:** `.github/workflows/deploy.yml:139-151` · **Area:** deploy-infra

**Root cause:** The `changes` job unconditionally retags registry `:latest-{web,api}` as `:previous-*` at the start of every push. But images are pushed to `:latest-*` BEFORE the Trivy gate (build-push at deploy.yml:236-241 with `push: true`, Trivy at 260-267) and before smoke/health validation. When a deploy fails AFTER the push (Trivy finding, smoke failure, health failure without rollback push-back races), registry `:latest-*` points at an image that never ran in production. The NEXT push then retags that unvetted image as `:previous-*`, and if that deploy's health gate fails, auto-rollback (deploy.yml:842-861) deploys the never-shipped image to prod. This precondition is not hypothetical: the APT_CACHE_…

**Impact:** After any push-then-blocked deploy, the auto-rollback target is an image that never passed smoke/health (possibly functionally broken, definitely CVE-gated). A subsequent failed deploy then 'rolls back' to it, potentially converting a bad deploy into a worse one.

**Recommended fix:** Move the :previous retag to AFTER the health gate passes (e.g. final step of the ship job: retag the image that was just verified live), or retag from a `:shipped` tag that only the green path writes — so :previous always means 'last image that actually served traffic'.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced the full chain from the workflow code. (1) The `changes` job unconditionally retags registry :latest-{web,api} as :previous-* at the start of every push (deploy.yml:139-151). (2) Both build jobs push to :latest-* BEFORE their Trivy gate (web: push at 235-241, scan at 260-267; api: push at 283-289, scan at 302-310, exit-code:'1'). (3) When Trivy (or smoke) fails after the push, the ship job is skipped (deploy.yml:500-503 requires smoke success), and the auto-rollback is a STEP inside that skipped job (deploy.yml:830-831) — so nothing repairs registry state: :latest-* is left pointing at a never-deployed, unvetted image while prod keeps running the old one. (4) The NEXT push's `changes` job then retags that unvetted image as :previous-*, destroying the only tag on the actually-shipped image; if that deploy fails its health gate, auto-rollback (deploy.yml:842-861) pulls and deploys the never-shipped image to prod. The authors demonstrably understood this poisoning mechanic for the health-failure path — the comment at deploy.yml:852-860 exists precisely to keep registry :latest aligned so the next retag doesn't poison :previous — but the Trivy/smoke-failure path has no equivalent fix because no job runs at all. No :shipped tag or previous-validation exists (smoke only ever tests :latest-*, deploy/README.md:379-393 assumes :previous = prior :latest). The precondition is documented as real, not hypothetical: apps/api/Dockerfile:62-67 records CVE-2026-45447 (2026-06-10) "failed every deploy until the cache rotated" — i.e., consecutive Trivy-blocked-after-push runs actually happened, each leaving an unvetted image on :latest-*. There is also a second, more direct consequence of the same push-before-gate root cause: after a Trivy-blocked api push, a web-only follow-up push skips build-api entirely (Trivy only re-scans rebuilt variants), smoke boots the CVE-gated :latest-api successfully (deploy.yml:407-484 — the block was CVE-based, not functional), and ship deploys it straight to prod (deploy.yml:678,712) with all gates green. Severity stays medium: the rollback-promotes-never-shipped-image path requires two consecutive failures of specific kinds (post-push gate failure, then a health failure on the following deploy), and in the common Trivy-CVE case the promoted image still boots; the direct-ship variant ships a functional-but-CVE-gated image. Real and impactful — it corrupts the rollback safety net exactly when deploys are flaky — but bounded/edge, not a normal-use pilot outage (not high) and not auth-bypass/data-loss (not critical). The proposed fix (retag :previous only after the health gate passes, or retag from a green-path-only :shipped tag) is sound.

### 40. Deterministic jobIds collide with 7-day-retained failed jobs, silently neutering the workflow-waiting sweeper in a compound DB-outage scenario

**Severity:** medium (finder said low) · **Location:** `apps/api/src/lib/sweepers/workflow-waiting.ts:104-144` · **Area:** queues-jobs

**Root cause:** BullMQ's add-with-custom-jobId is a no-op while ANY job hash with that id still exists — including one in the FAILED set (verified in bullmq 5.76.10: addDelayedJob-6.lua:84-88 does `if EXISTS jobIdKey then return handleDuplicatedJob`, and removeOnFail keeps failed workflow jobs for 7 days / 5000 count per lib/workflows/queue.ts:94). The waiting sweeper recovers stranded runs by re-enqueueing the SAME deterministic ids: `run-${runId}` (queue.ts:112) and `resume-${runId}-${waitSeq}` where waitSeq = stepLog.length, unchanged on a stuck run (workflow-waiting.ts:122-124). Stranding chain: a resume/run job exhausts its 3 attempts during a DB outage; the worker's failed-listener calls failRunFromRe…

**Impact:** Requires a DB outage spanning ~6-14s (3 exponential-backoff attempts) plus the exhaustion-stamp also failing — a plausible single incident, not two. Result: the run is stranded in a non-terminal state for up to 7 days with the recovery mechanism reporting success. Bounded blast radius (workflow runs only; webhook deliveries are immune since their orphan query excludes failedAt-stamped rows).

**Recommended fix:** In the sweeper, before re-enqueueing, `queue.getJob(jobId)` and if its state is 'failed', `job.retry()` (or remove + re-add) instead of a blind add. Alternatively have the sweeper call failRunFromRetryExhaustion for runs it has re-enqueued N consecutive ticks without progress.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced the full chain. (1) Failed workflow jobs are retained 7 days under their deterministic ids (queue.ts:94 removeOnFail age 7d/count 5000; ids run-${runId} at queue.ts:112, resume-${runId}-${waitSeq} at queue.ts:142). (2) In the installed bullmq@5.76.10, both addStandardJob-9.lua:89-93 and addDelayedJob-6.lua:84-88 do `if rcall("EXISTS", jobIdKey) == 1 then return handleDuplicatedJob(...)`, and handleDuplicatedJob.lua only emits a 'duplicated' event and returns the existing id — a silent no-op, so a re-add against a retained FAILED job does nothing and throws nothing. (3) If a DB outage spans a job's 3-attempt retry window (~6-14s, exponential backoff base 2s) the worker's failed listener (worker.ts:195-200) calls failRunFromRetryExhaustion, whose entire body is try/catch+console.warn (runner.ts:869-895); with the DB still down the failure stamp is swallowed and the run stays non-terminal (queued/waiting). (4) The waiting sweeper then recomputes the IDENTICAL jobId every 60s — runner.ts:515-541 persists stepLog (with the pushed waiting entry) before enqueueing with stepLog.length, so workflow-waiting.ts:122-124's waitSeq matches the failed job's; queued runs re-add run-${runId} (workflow-waiting.ts:137). Every re-add silently no-ops while the sweeper logs 're-enqueued N stranded run(s)' success (workflow-waiting.ts:148-157). (5) No alternate recovery exists: zero uses of getFailed/job.retry/queue.clean/obliterate in apps/api/src; only outs are an ask_question contact reply (different jobId inbound-${runId}-${tag}) or the lazy 7-day removeOnFail trim, after which the run resumes ~7 days late. Concrete impact: any workflow run whose job exhausts retries during a short Postgres outage (e.g. VPS reboot where postgres comes up slower than the api worker, or a postgres OOM/restart) is stranded non-terminal for up to 7 days while the recovery mechanism falsely reports success, then may fire a days-late automation step. Severity corrected low→medium per the stated bar: this is a real correctness failure of a recovery path that masks itself (not polish), but bounded/edge — single-incident compound trigger, workflow-runs-only blast radius, eventual self-heal, no data loss or double customer send (run lock + stale-resume guards still hold on eventual resume). Not by_design: no doc or memory entry covers this; the sweeper's own comments (workflow-waiting.ts:25-28, queue.ts:126) assume the dedupe no-op only happens 'while the original delayed job is still alive', showing the failed-set collision was not a considered tradeoff.

### 41. Inbound media download failure is a permanent downgrade — a transient UploadThing/Meta blip strips media with no delayed retry, despite Meta retaining the binary ~30 days

**Severity:** medium (finder said low) · **Location:** `apps/api/src/webhooks/meta/meta.controller.ts:344-376` · **Area:** reliability-failure

**Root cause:** The 2-phase async inbound media design (locked decision) handles failure by permanently clearing the row's media columns: when the detached download fails after its bounded in-request retry, completePendingMedia nulls mediaKind/mediaKey/etc. and emits an empty media_ready frame (meta.controller.ts:355-375); the 60s sweeper does the same for any row still pending after 2 minutes (inbound-media.ts:90-102 — 'Retry semantics: we just CLEAR the stale media columns'). There is no deferred/scheduled re-attempt, even though the Meta media id remains available in `rawPayload` and Meta's CDN retains inbound media for ~30 days — so an UploadThing outage (or Meta CDN blip) lasting longer than the per-re…

**Impact:** During any blob-storage or Meta-CDN outage window, customer-sent media (photos of the problem, voice notes, documents) is silently and permanently lost to the inbox even though the bytes remain fetchable from Meta for ~30 days. Bounded by outage duration and pilot volume.

**Recommended fix:** Instead of clearing immediately, park failed rows (e.g. keep mediaKind + the Meta media id from rawPayload) and have the existing inbound-media sweeper attempt one re-download per row over a longer horizon (e.g. hourly for 24h) before the final text-only downgrade — reuses the sweeper that already runs every 60s, no new infrastructure.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced the full chain from code. The ONLY retry for inbound media is in-request: 3 attempts x 250ms exponential backoff on the Meta fetch and 3 on the blob upload (meta.controller.ts:503-531), deliberately bounded to stay inside Meta's ~10s webhook window — total horizon ~1-3s. On exhaustion the outcome is { ok: false } (:595) and completePendingMedia's failure branch permanently nulls every media column (mediaKind/mediaKey/mediaUrl/etc., :356-368) and emits an empty message.media_ready frame (:370-375). The 60s sweeper does the same to any row still pending after 2 minutes and its own doc-comment states 'Retry semantics: we just CLEAR the stale media columns' (inbound-media.ts:18-20, 90-102). No deferred/scheduled re-download exists anywhere (grepped: only the sweeper is wired in workflow-worker.service.ts:121), the media controller 404s on null mediaUrl (media.controller.ts:74-82), and nothing ever reads the Meta media id back out of rawPayload — even though the failure branch leaves rawPayload intact and Meta retains the binary ~30 days. Not by_design: the locked decision covers the 2-phase async architecture (fast 200, detached download, shimmer-to-swap, sweeper backstop), not terminal loss after 3 quick attempts; the code comment at :497-501 itself says 'without retry, a single blip permanently strands the media... there's no second chance', showing the authors considered transient stranding a bug — the shipped mitigation just has a horizon (seconds) far shorter than a realistic UploadThing/Meta-CDN incident (minutes). Concrete impact: during any blob-store or Meta-CDN outage longer than a few seconds, all customer-sent photos/voice-notes/documents arriving in that window are silently and irreversibly downgraded to text-only (caption-less media becomes an empty bubble) in a support product whose customers send 'photos of the problem'. Severity corrected low->medium per the bar: not polish (permanent degradation of customer content), but bounded/edge — requires an external outage to coincide with inbound media, body/caption survive, customers can resend, and rawPayload preserves the media id so manual recovery is possible for ~30 days. Same class as the deferred-MEDIUM fairness gaps. The proposed fix (park failed rows, let the existing sweeper re-attempt over a longer horizon before the final downgrade) is feasible with no new infrastructure.

### 42. Documented 'pre-pilot hardening' sudo allowlist is out of lockstep with deploy.yml — applying it silently disables the Caddyfile.prev snapshot (|| true) and breaks the rollback's Caddyfile restore

**Severity:** medium (finder said low) · **Location:** `deploy/README.md:434-446` · **Area:** deploy-infra

**Root cause:** The README's pre-pilot sudoers allowlist (which it instructs to apply 'BEFORE accepting real traffic') lists mv/mkdir/chown/validate/reload/disable/daemon-reload/rm — but the workflow has since grown two more sudo calls it doesn't cover: the pre-overwrite snapshot `sudo cp -a /etc/caddy/Caddyfile /etc/caddy/Caddyfile.prev` (deploy.yml:622) and the rollback restore `sudo cp -a /etc/caddy/Caddyfile.prev /etc/caddy/Caddyfile` (deploy.yml:872). The snapshot line ends in `2>/dev/null || true`, so once the allowlist is applied the snapshot fails SILENTLY on every deploy — Caddyfile.prev freezes at its pre-hardening state forever — while the rollback's cp (no || true, under set -euo pipefail) hard-…

**Impact:** Operator follows the repo's own pre-pilot hardening doc → every subsequent deploy silently stops snapshotting the proxy config, and the first auto-rollback that needs the Caddyfile restore either fails red mid-rollback or restores an ancient config.

**Recommended fix:** Add the two cp invocations to the README allowlist (`/usr/bin/cp -a /etc/caddy/Caddyfile /etc/caddy/Caddyfile.prev` and `/usr/bin/cp -a /etc/caddy/Caddyfile.prev /etc/caddy/Caddyfile`), and drop `2>/dev/null || true` from deploy.yml:622 so a snapshot failure fails the deploy loudly instead of silently disarming the rollback.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced from the code. The README's pre-pilot sudoers allowlist (deploy/README.md:437-445, instructed as mandatory "BEFORE accepting real traffic" at :419) covers exactly 8 sudo commands; I cross-checked all 11 sudo invocations in .github/workflows/deploy.yml and the only two NOT covered are the Caddyfile snapshot cp (:622) and the rollback restore cp (:872) — both added after the allowlist was written. The snapshot at :622 ends in `2>/dev/null || true`, so once the allowlist is applied, sudo's password-required failure is fully suppressed and every deploy silently stops snapshotting — directly contradicting the README's own lockstep promise at :454-455 that a mismatch "will fail with `a password is required`" (true for the other 9 calls, false for this one). The rollback restore at :872 runs under `set -euo pipefail` (:843) with no fallback: if a stale Caddyfile.prev exists the step dies red AFTER the image swap (:845-864) but BEFORE the Caddyfile revert and the migration-warning echo (:889); if Caddyfile.prev never existed the `[ -f ]` guard (:871) silently skips the revert. Either way the exact failure the workflow's own comments call out (:616-621, :865-870 — bad deploy changes both images and Caddyfile → reverting images alone leaves the site dark) is silently disarmed. One imprecision in the finding: the "restores an ancient config" branch cannot occur (the cp fails outright, restoring nothing), but that doesn't change the verdict. Severity corrected low→medium: this is a real correctness gap that disarms a documented rollback safety mechanism via the repo's own mandated hardening step and manifests mid-incident, potentially prolonging an outage — but it is bounded (requires hardening applied + a failed deploy triggering auto-rollback + a Caddyfile change in that deploy for worst case; image rollback still completes), so it stays below high. Not a locked decision — the sudo allowlist is the repo's own recommended hardening, and the doc itself mandates lockstep maintenance that the repo currently violates.

### 43. api container's migrate-then-serve boot depends on the npm registry at runtime: corepack must download pnpm as the `node` user on every freshly created container

**Severity:** medium (finder said low) · **Location:** `apps/api/Dockerfile:19, 91, 119` · **Area:** deploy-infra

**Root cause:** `corepack prepare pnpm@10.33.3 --activate` runs as root at build time, caching pnpm under /root/.cache/node/corepack (mode 700, unreadable by the runtime user). The image then switches to `USER node` (line 91), and the compose CMD override runs `pnpm prisma migrate deploy` before exec'ing node (docker-compose.yml:413). On the first boot of every newly CREATED container (i.e. immediately after each deploy's recreate), the pnpm shim finds an empty corepack cache at /home/node/.cache and downloads pnpm@10.33.3 from registry.npmjs.org before the migration can run. Restarts of the same container reuse the now-populated cache, so the exposure is the post-deploy first boot — but that includes the a…

**Impact:** npmjs/DNS unavailability at deploy or rollback time turns into an api crash-loop at the migrate step (exit before exec, restart: unless-stopped retries) — an external runtime dependency in the boot path of an image that is otherwise fully self-contained. Also adds a few seconds and a network fetch to every post-deploy first boot.

**Recommended fix:** Warm the cache for the runtime user at build time: after `USER node` add `RUN pnpm --version` (populates /home/node/.cache/node/corepack in a layer), or set `ENV COREPACK_HOME=/opt/corepack` before the root-stage `corepack prepare` so the cache is world-readable. Alternatively invoke the prisma CLI binary directly (`node_modules/.bin/prisma migrate deploy`) and drop pnpm from the boot path entirely.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced on the actual built api image, not just from reading code. apps/api/Dockerfile:19 runs `corepack enable && corepack prepare pnpm@10.33.3 --activate` as root, caching pnpm under /root/.cache/node/corepack (verified present in the image; /root is mode 700). Dockerfile:91 switches to USER node, whose corepack cache (/home/node/.cache) is empty — verified absent in the image. No COREPACK_HOME is set anywhere (grepped Dockerfiles, docker-compose.yml, deploy.yml). The compose CMD override (docker-compose.yml:409-414) runs `(cd /app && pnpm prisma migrate deploy) && exec node ...`, so the corepack pnpm shim (/usr/local/bin/pnpm -> corepack/dist/pnpm.js, verified) is in the boot path. Empirical test 1: `docker run --network none` as the node user → corepack attempts to download pnpm-10.33.3.tgz from registry.npmjs.org and throws (EAI_AGAIN), exiting before the migration — under restart: unless-stopped this is a crash-loop until the registry/DNS is reachable. Empirical test 2: with network, the download succeeds and caches to /home/node/.cache/node/corepack/v1/pnpm/10.33.3 in the container writable layer — reused on restarts, lost on recreate. deploy.yml:414 (`docker compose up -d`) recreates the api container on every deploy, and auto-rollback recreates from :previous-* images with the identical flaw, so a registry/DNS outage during a deploy window blocks BOTH forward deploy and rollback simultaneously (the dual failure is what lifts this above low/polish; the Jan-2025 corepack signature-key incident is real-world precedent for exactly this class). Not by_design: nothing in CLAUDE.md or the Dockerfile treats the runtime registry dependency as deliberate — the Dockerfile comments treat the image as self-contained and even strip npm as dead weight. Severity corrected low → medium: real availability hazard in the boot path, but bounded to deploy/recreate windows, never affects an already-running container, and self-heals when the registry returns — does not reach the 'outage a pilot hits in normal use' bar for high. The proposed fixes are viable: /app/node_modules/.bin/prisma exists in the image (verified, mode 755), so pnpm can be dropped from the boot path; or warm /home/node's cache with a post-USER `RUN pnpm --version`; or set COREPACK_HOME to a world-readable path before the root-stage prepare.


## LOW (43)

### 1. Settings IA: the same pages carry three different names across nav surfaces, and two different pages both present as 'Team settings'

**Severity:** low (finder said medium) · **Location:** `apps/web/src/components/layouts/settings-sub-sidebar.tsx:52-135` · **Area:** ux-screens

**Root cause:** Cross-referencing the three settings nav surfaces: (1) /settings index page (the AppRail 'Settings' target) is titled 'Team settings' (settings/page.tsx:13 metadata, :68 h1) and shows Snippets/Tags/Stages/Contact-fields cards — but the sidebar item labeled 'Team settings' points at a DIFFERENT page, /settings/team, whose h1 is just 'Team' (team-settings.tsx:267). (2) Sidebar item 'User settings' → /settings/workspace whose h1/metadata is 'Account settings' (workspace/page.tsx:14, 64), while the sidebar's own title is 'Workspace settings' (settings-sub-sidebar.tsx:52) — so 'workspace' names the whole section AND a page that calls itself 'Account'. (3) The stages page is 'Lifecycle' in the sid…

**Impact:** Daily-use confusion for a pilot team: 'Team settings' resolves to two different destinations depending on which nav you use; an admin told to 'open Stages' won't find that word in the sidebar; 'Channels' vs 'WhatsApp' vs 'WhatsApp connection' for the single most important onboarding page. Settings discoverability is the screen set new admins explore first.

**Recommended fix:** Pick one noun per page and use it everywhere: rename the sidebar items to match each page's h1 ('Account', 'Team', 'Stages', 'WhatsApp'), retitle the /settings index to something unique (e.g. 'Inbox configuration') or give it a sidebar entry, and reserve 'workspace' for nothing or for the sidebar title only.

> Verifier (high confidence): Every cited label was independently verified in the code and the naming collisions are real: (1) the AppRail "Settings" entry (app-rail.tsx:219-223) lands on /settings, whose metadata and h1 are both "Team settings" (settings/page.tsx:13, 68), while the sidebar item literally labeled "Team settings" (settings-sub-sidebar.tsx:61-62) points at a DIFFERENT page, /settings/team, whose h1 is just "Team" (team-settings.tsx:267) — so the same name resolves to two distinct destinations depending on which nav surface is used, and the /settings index page itself has no sidebar entry (no item highlights when on it). (2) The sidebar section is titled "Workspace settings" (line 52) yet the item "User settings" (lines 55-56) routes to /settings/workspace whose page calls itself "Account settings" (workspace/page.tsx:14, 64) — three names (workspace/user/account) for overlapping concepts. (3) Stages carries four names: sidebar "Lifecycle" (line 97), page h1 "Customer stages" (stages-settings.tsx:242), browser-tab metadata "Stages · Settings" (stages/page.tsx:8), and index card "Stages" (settings/page.tsx:46). (4) The WhatsApp connection page is "Channels" in the sidebar (line 80), "WhatsApp" on the workspace card (workspace/page.tsx:46), and "WhatsApp connection" as its h1 (whatsapp-settings.tsx:125). No locked decision or memory entry covers settings naming; the doc comments show the two-landing-page split is deliberate but the label drift is not — workspace/page.tsx's own comment refers to /settings as "Team settings" while the sidebar applies that exact label to /settings/team, which is drift, not design. Severity corrected medium→low: this is genuine daily-use IA confusion for a pilot admin, but purely labeling polish — no page is unreachable, no functionality is hidden or broken, no data/security/correctness impact; worst case is a misdirected click and momentary confusion.

### 2. ENCRYPTION_KEY (envelope master key) is injected into and required by the web container that never uses it

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `docker-compose.yml:199-201` · **Area:** crypto-secrets

**Root cause:** The Next.js `app` service receives `ENCRYPTION_KEY: ${ENCRYPTION_KEY}` (docker-compose.yml:201), and packages/config/src/index.ts lists ENCRYPTION_KEY in the unconditional `required` array (lines 44-51) which `validateEnv("web")` enforces from apps/web/instrumentation-node.ts:20 — yet nothing in apps/web imports the envelope module (grep over apps/web/src finds only a comment in whatsapp-settings.tsx:35; all encryptSecret/decryptSecret call sites live in apps/api). WhatsappService's own doc-comment confirms the migration removed the need: "the envelope key no longer needs to live in the web image" (whatsapp.service.ts:98-99). The key currently lives in two containers' environments (visible to anyone who can inspect either container).

**Impact:** A compromise of the Next.js container (the larger, internet-facing render surface) yields the master key for every tenant's Meta credentials and API keys (web also holds DATABASE_URL, so key + ciphertext = full decrypt). Halving the key's exposure surface is free.

**Recommended fix:** Move ENCRYPTION_KEY from `required` to `apiRequired` in packages/config/src/index.ts and delete the line from the web service's compose environment (and from the web image's runtime expectations). One-line each; no code change in apps/web.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced every link in the claim: the web container is injected with ENCRYPTION_KEY (docker-compose.yml:201), validateEnv("web") hard-requires it at boot because it sits in the unconditional `required` array rather than `apiRequired` (packages/config/src/index.ts:44-51, called from apps/web/instrumentation-node.ts:20), yet a repo-wide grep shows every encryptSecret/decryptSecret/ENCRYPTION_KEY code reference lives in apps/api/src — apps/web contains only a prose comment (whatsapp-settings.tsx:35). The api's own doc-comment (whatsapp.service.ts:97-99) states the NestJS getConfig endpoint replaced the old RSC page that imported decryptSecret into apps/web, "which is why the envelope key no longer needs to live in the web image" — i.e., this is acknowledged post-Phase-5 vestige, not a locked decision (no CLAUDE.md/memory entry defends it; the locked encryption-at-rest items concern column scope, not key placement). Impact is real but bounded: it is pure least-privilege hardening. Exploiting it requires first compromising the web container, which already exposes DATABASE_URL + BETTER_AUTH_SECRET (session forgery + full DB read); the key adds decryption of all tenants' Meta credentials/API-key secrets on top of an already-catastrophic compromise, at one pilot tenant. Severity low is correct; the fix is two one-liners (move ENCRYPTION_KEY to apiRequired + delete the web compose line) and the compose line cannot be removed alone or web crash-loops on validateEnv — matching the known 'deploy env vars must be wired in compose' failure class in reverse.

### 3. Documented plaintext-row re-encryption script does not exist; no key-rotation tooling despite prod fail-hard on plaintext

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `.env.example:48-53` · **Area:** crypto-secrets

**Root cause:** .env.example instructs: "After a fresh deploy with an existing DB, run `docker compose exec app npx tsx scripts/encrypt-legacy-secrets.ts` to rewrite any plaintext rows" — but scripts/ contains only pg-backup.sh and a repo-wide search finds no encrypt-legacy-secrets.ts anywhere. Meanwhile envelope-core.ts:56-62 makes decryptSecret THROW in production on any un-prefixed (plaintext) secret row, and envelope.ts:11-13 prescribes key rotation "via a migration that re-encrypts" — tooling that also doesn't exist. If a plaintext row ever reaches prod (raw-SQL backfill, restore of a pre-encryption dump), the webhook path silently 403s Meta (config.ts:291-298 returns null on decrypt throw) and the documented remediation doesn't exist.

**Impact:** An operator following the repo's own runbook on a restored/legacy DB hits a missing file during an incident; ENCRYPTION_KEY rotation is effectively impossible without writing new tooling under pressure (every team's WhatsApp integration breaks until each admin re-pastes credentials).

**Recommended fix:** Either write the small script (iterate ChannelConnection.secrets, OutboundWebhook.secret, Workflow.triggerConfig.secret + graph bearerTokens; skip isEncrypted; encryptSecret the rest) or delete the stale instruction and document re-paste-via-UI as the only remediation. For rotation, the same script with old-key-decrypt/new-key-encrypt covers it.

> Verifier (high confidence): Every factual claim verifies, and git history confirms the root cause: scripts/encrypt-legacy-secrets.ts was added in commit 9739fae (one-shot re-encryption of plaintext Team-row Meta secrets) and DELETED in a113ff9/ed3cbdf when the schema moved secrets from Team columns to ChannelConnection.secrets — but the .env.example runbook line (which also still says secrets live "in the Team row", and points at the `app` compose service, which is the WEB container) was never updated. The prod fail-hard is real: envelope-core.ts throws on un-prefixed values in production, config.ts swallows the throw and returns null, and meta.controller.ts then 403s both webhook verify and POST — so a plaintext row in prod silently kills that team's WhatsApp ingest, exactly as the finding states. No rotation tooling exists despite envelope.ts:11-13 prescribing "rotate via a migration that re-encrypts", and envelope.ts:15-20 still documents plaintext passthrough as tolerated, contradicting the core's prod throw. This is NOT covered by any locked decision (the encryption-at-rest lock concerns Message.body/Contact.phoneNumber scope, not this tooling). Severity stays LOW, not medium/high: no normal code path can produce a plaintext secret row in prod (the only write path, whatsapp.service.ts upsert, always encryptSecret()s; the current ChannelConnection schema post-dates encryption so dumps contain ciphertext), the trigger requires an operator-initiated edge event (raw-SQL backfill, pre-encryption dump restore, or key rotation nobody is planning — docs say write-once), and a real remediation exists outside the stale doc: re-pasting credentials in /settings/whatsapp rewrites them encrypted. Fix is a one-line doc correction or a small rewritten script against the new schema.

### 4. Workflow http_request customHeaders are stored plaintext at rest and returned unredacted, unlike the sibling bearerToken field

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `apps/api/src/lib/workflows/steps/http-request.ts:71-77` · **Area:** crypto-secrets

**Root cause:** The http_request step encrypts and redacts `bearerToken` (encryptGraphStepSecrets, workflows.service.ts:1082-1088, redacts to `bearerTokenSet: true` at http-request.ts:73), but `customHeaders` — the field a customer will use for `X-API-Key: <secret>` or a custom `Authorization` header when their target API doesn't use Bearer auth — passes through both layers verbatim: stored plaintext in the Workflow.graph JSONB and echoed back in full by redactConfig (http-request.ts:74 `if (config.customHeaders) out.customHeaders = config.customHeaders`). The encryptGraphStepSecrets dispatch comment (workflows.service.ts:1061-1064) explicitly says to extend the table when a step ships a new secret field, but customHeaders was missed.

**Impact:** Third-party API credentials placed in custom headers (a normal integration pattern) sit unencrypted in the database and its pg_dump backups (/opt/ccp/backups), inconsistent with the system's own claim that credentials are envelope-encrypted at rest.

**Recommended fix:** Encrypt customHeaders VALUES in encryptGraphStepSecrets (same isEncrypted-idempotent pattern as bearerToken), decrypt in the step's run(), and redact values to header-name-only (or `<set>`) in redactConfig — the secret-merge-on-PATCH logic in workflows.service.ts:193-205 already provides the preserve-when-omitted machinery to copy.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced both halves of the claim. (1) At rest: encryptGraphStepSecrets (apps/api/src/team/workflows/workflows.service.ts:1069-1093) envelope-encrypts ONLY http_request.bearerToken (lines 1082-1088); customHeaders pass through to the Workflow.graph JSONB plaintext on create (:134) and update (:226). (2) Echo: redactConfig (apps/api/src/lib/workflows/steps/http-request.ts:71-77) redacts bearerToken to bearerTokenSet:true but returns customHeaders verbatim (:74), and toDto→redactGraph (workflows.service.ts:988) is the only GET sanitization. run() decrypts only bearerToken (:106-114) and uses customHeaders raw (:119-123), confirming no decrypt path exists for them. This is NOT covered by the locked no-encryption-at-rest decision — that covers customer data; the system's stated posture is that credentials ARE envelope-encrypted (Team.metaAppSecret, TeamApiKey.secret, bearerToken, incoming-webhook secret), and customHeaders is the natural home for X-API-Key / non-Bearer Authorization credentials when a target API doesn't use Bearer auth. No code comment or memory file documents customHeaders as a deliberate exclusion; the dispatch-table comment (:1060-1064) explicitly invites extension for new secret fields. Severity stays LOW, not medium: exploitation requires DB/backup compromise (which by accepted design already exposes all plaintext customer data), and the unredacted echo is bounded to team members with workflow capability who could exfiltrate the header anyway by retargeting the step URL — so it is a defense-in-depth inconsistency with the bearerToken sibling, not a normal-operation bug. Note one friction the fix must handle: the web editor (apps/web/src/features/workflows/components/builder/step-editors.tsx:1447) round-trip-edits customHeaders as plain text, so value-level redaction needs the bearerTokenSet-style merge-on-PATCH pattern (workflows.service.ts:193-205) extended per header key.

### 5. ENCRYPTION_KEY is validated presence-only at boot; a malformed key ships through CI smoke green and fails only at first secret use

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `packages/config/src/index.ts:44-51, 104-106` · **Area:** crypto-secrets

**Root cause:** validateEnv checks only `Boolean(process.env[c.name])` (index.ts:104-106) for ENCRYPTION_KEY; the base64/32-byte shape check lives in loadKey (envelope-core.ts:26-32) which is LAZY — it runs on the first encryptSecret/decryptSecret call, not at boot. The deploy pipeline believes otherwise: deploy.yml:367-370 says "Real secrets are used so the smoke test catches any value-specific issues (encryption-key length, ...)" — but the smoke test only polls container health and `/health` JSON (deploy.yml:407-484), neither of which ever calls loadKey. A truncated/typo'd ENCRYPTION_KEY GH secret therefore deploys green; the first symptom is webhooks going dark (getMetaWebhookConfig catches the decrypt throw and returns null, silently 403ing Meta).

**Impact:** A bad master key reaches production as a green deploy and manifests as silently-403'd Meta webhooks / undecryptable credentials hours later — the exact failure mode the fail-fast env validation philosophy (index.ts:11-14) exists to prevent.

**Recommended fix:** In validateEnv's api branch, decode ENCRYPTION_KEY and exit(1) unless it base64-decodes to exactly 32 bytes (3 lines, mirrors the TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS check). That also makes the deploy.yml smoke-test comment true, since the api container would crash-loop in smoke.

> Verifier (high confidence): Every element of the finding reproduces from the code. (1) validateEnv checks ENCRYPTION_KEY presence-only: packages/config/src/index.ts:104-106 (`present(c) = Boolean(process.env[c.name])`); ENCRYPTION_KEY sits in the `required` list (index.ts:44-51) whose hint even says "Must be base64 of 32 random bytes" — yet no shape check exists, while the SAME function shape-validates TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS (index.ts:174-184), META_GRAPH_VERSION (index.ts:190-199), and six numeric tunables (index.ts:204-225). So the omission is an inconsistency, not a pattern. (2) The 32-byte check is lazy: apps/api/src/lib/crypto/envelope-core.ts:17-35 — loadKey throws on `decoded.length !== 32` (lines 27-32) but is only invoked from encryptSecret (line 38) and decryptSecret (line 65). (3) No boot path touches it: every call site is request/job-time (whatsapp.service.ts:163/270-271, providers/config.ts:196/287, outbound-webhooks/worker.ts:362, workflows.service.ts:669/1056/1085, http-request.ts:108); the health controller (apps/api/src/health/health.controller.ts) only pings Postgres+Redis; prisma/seeds/* never call encryptSecret; main.ts only calls validateEnv. (4) The deploy pipeline's claim is false: deploy.yml:369-370 says the smoke test "catches any value-specific issues (encryption-key length, ...)" but the smoke step (deploy.yml:407-484) only checks Docker health status + greps `"ok":true` from /health, and the post-ship gate (deploy.yml:722-787) likewise only curls health URLs — none of which exercise loadKey. The pre-deploy GH-secrets check (deploy.yml:87-128) tests non-empty only. So a truncated/typo'd ENCRYPTION_KEY ships fully green and first manifests at runtime — getMetaWebhookConfig's decrypt failure maps to silent 403s on inbound Meta webhooks (the code itself documents this failure mode at providers/config.ts:249: "rotated ENCRYPTION_KEY ... the dispatcher maps that to 403 silently"), plus failed outbound sends. Not by_design: no locked decision covers env-shape validation, and the project has precedent for exactly this class (the 2026-05-16 ENCRYPTION_KEY-missing incident, deploy.yml:86, which motivated the presence check). Severity stays LOW: the current production key is valid, so this is a latent guard gap that only fires on a future operator misconfiguration (key rotation typo, re-pasted GH secret) — real, fail-fast-philosophy-violating (index.ts:11-14), with a misleading comment that could lull an operator during a rotation, but not a bug a pilot hits in normal use. The proposed 3-line fix in validateEnv's api branch mirrors the existing TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS pattern and would also make the deploy.yml comment true (the smoke api container would crash-loop at boot).

### 6. Contact_customFields_gin_idx (jsonb_path_ops) is unusable by every customFields query in the codebase — dead write overhead protected as load-bearing

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `prisma/migrations/0_init/migration.sql:827` · **Area:** db-queries-indexes

**Root cause:** [Merged from two duplicate findings, one filed under db-schema and one under db-queries-indexes — same root cause.] The hand-written GIN index `Contact_customFields_gin_idx` uses the `jsonb_path_ops` operator class, which serves ONLY `@>`, `@?`, `@@` operators. A repo-wide grep finds zero uses of any of those on Contact.customFields. The three actual query shapes are: (1) quick-search `c."customFields"::text ILIKE '%..%'` (apps/api/src/lib/queries/contacts.ts:135 — its own comment admits the index-miss), (2) field filter `c."customFields" ->> key ILIKE '%..%'` (contacts.ts:141 — extraction, no GIN class can serve it), and (3) key-strip on field-definition delete `"customFields" ? key` (apps/api/src/team/contact-fields/contact-fields.service.ts:238 — the `?` operator requires the default `jsonb_ops` class). The code's own comment at contacts.ts:122-127 correctly states 'the jsonb::text ILIKE can't use the customFields jsonb_path_ops GIN index (that index serves containment ops @>/?/, not substring match)'. A repo-wide grep for `@>` finds only those explanatory comments; no service, worker, or query uses a containment predicate.

**Impact:** Every Contact UPDATE pays GIN maintenance for an index no query can use — and Contact updates are hot (ingest bumps lastInboundAt on every inbound message; lastInboundAt is itself indexed so those updates are never HOT, meaning the GIN gets touched each time; plus each custom-field edit, CSV import row, ingest revive). Ironically this is the exact write-amplification cost the Contact.customFields comment (schema.prisma:575-579) cites to justify NOT adding indexes — plus the ongoing migration-hygiene tax of hand-protecting the index from Prisma's drift detector (the documented 'single-permanent-diff' / strip-spurious-DROPs process).

**Recommended fix:** Confirm with EXPLAIN on a populated DB that no plan uses the index, then drop it in a new migration, correct the three comments (schema.prisma generator block, Contact.customFields note, contacts.ts:124), and delete the protect-it-from-migrate-dev process notes / project memory that calls this index the 'single-permanent-diff'. Alternatively, if exact-key/value containment lookups (`customFields @> '{"plan":"pro"}'`) are genuinely planned, recreate it as default `jsonb_ops` so the `?` key-strip query and future `@>` filters can use it — but as of today, dropping is the honest move; the current ILIKE field-filter will never use it either way.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced every step of the claim. The hand-written GIN index uses jsonb_path_ops, which serves only @>, @?, @@ (NOT the key-exists ? operator). A repo-wide grep across apps/api, apps/web, packages finds zero uses of @>, @?, or @@ on Contact.customFields — the only hits are two comments. The three real query shapes are (1) customFields::text ILIKE (quick-search, index-miss admitted in its own comment), (2) customFields ->> key ILIKE (extraction, unservable by any jsonb GIN opclass), (3) customFields ? key (key-strip on field-definition delete — servable by default jsonb_ops but NOT by jsonb_path_ops). So the index is unusable by every query in the codebase. The write-overhead mechanism also checks out: Contact_teamId_lastInboundAt_idx exists, so the ingest path's per-inbound-message lastInboundAt bump changes an indexed column → never HOT → inserts entries into ALL Contact indexes including this dead GIN. Not by_design: no locked decision covers this index; its documented rationale (schema.prisma:576 'exact-key lookups') is factually unfulfillable with jsonb_path_ops, and contacts.ts:124 wrongly lists ? among the ops it serves — the protection (migration header 'permanent', memory 'single-permanent-diff') is premised on a misconception, not a deliberate tradeoff. Severity low is correct: no user-visible bug, bounded write amplification at pilot scale; the cost is dead GIN maintenance on a hot-write table plus misleading docs/memory that prevent cleanup. Fix direction (EXPLAIN-confirm then drop, or recreate as jsonb_ops if exact-key lookups are genuinely planned) is sound.

### 7. Six User-referencing FK columns on large/growing tables lack a usable index for the ON DELETE SET NULL/CASCADE trigger scan — including answeredByUserId, missed by the very migration that cited the rationale

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `prisma/schema.prisma:826, 1279, 1703, 1850, 1898, 2093` · **Area:** db-schema

**Root cause:** User hard-delete is a live admin route (apps/api/src/users/users.service.ts:347 `await this.db.user.delete(...)`). On that delete, Postgres runs the referential-action scan (`WHERE <col> = $1`) on every child table. The team already established the pattern of indexing these columns on big tables — Message_senderUserId_idx (migration 20260602120000, comment: 'Without it, hard-deleting a teammate seq-scans the Message table') and Call_initiatedByUserId_idx (20260602130000, whose comment says the FK 'mirrors answeredByUserId') — but six columns on the larger remaining tables have no index that can serve the scan: Conversation.assignedUserId (schema.prisma:826; the only index is @@index([teamId, assignedUserId])), plus the columns listed in the evidence.

**Impact:** A single admin 'delete teammate' action runs up to six sequential seq-scans inside one delete transaction (FK triggers), holding row locks on the inbox's hottest tables (Conversation, ConversationEvent, InternalNote, TeamChannelMessage). At pilot scale this is milliseconds; at the 10^5-10^6-row scale the autovacuum migration itself anticipates, it becomes a multi-second blocking operation on the l…

**Recommended fix:** One additive migration mirroring 20260602120000: btree indexes on InternalNote(authorUserId), ConversationEvent(userId), TeamChannelMessage(authorUserId), TeamChannelReaction(userId), Call(answeredByUserId), Conversation(assignedUserId) — with matching @@index entries in schema.prisma so names stay drift-free. Cheap btrees; or consciously document that user hard-delete is accepted to be slow and skip the small tables (Snippet/Invite/Broadcast/AudienceGroup/TeamChannel/Pin/Member.addedById are fi…

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced every element of the claim. (1) User hard-delete is a live, reachable admin route: DELETE /api/users/:id (apps/api/src/users/users.controller.ts:151) calls `this.db.user.delete({ where: { id: targetId } })` at apps/api/src/users/users.service.ts:347, and the service doc-comment itself relies on "attribution survives via SetNull FKs" — so the FK referential-action scans definitely fire on this path. (2) The FKs are real DB-level constraints (no relationMode=prisma in schema.prisma; 0_init/migration.sql:1035,1044,1116 show ON DELETE SET NULL), so Postgres RI triggers run `UPDATE/DELETE ... WHERE <col> = $1` on each child table inside the delete transaction. (3) All six cited columns lack an index whose LEADING column is the FK column: Conversation.assignedUserId has only the composite (teamId, assignedUserId) — and the stack runs postgres:16.6-alpine (docker-compose.yml:11), which has no btree skip-scan (PG 18 feature), so a non-leading column can't serve the trigger scan; InternalNote.authorUserId, ConversationEvent.userId, TeamChannelMessage.authorUserId, TeamChannelReaction.userId (non-leading in its unique), and Call.answeredByUserId all have zero usable indexes. (4) The "team's own established pattern" claim is accurate: migration 20260602120000 added Message_senderUserId_idx explicitly to avoid this seq-scan, and 20260602130000 added Call_initiatedByUserId_idx with a comment literally saying it "mirrors answeredByUserId" — while never indexing answeredByUserId. That irony confirms this is an oversight, not a deliberate tradeoff; no locked decision covers FK-index coverage, and the 2026-06-02 migrations show the team's intent is the opposite of "accept the seq-scan". Severity LOW is correct: user hard-delete is a rare admin action; at pilot scale (single tenant, small tables) the six seq-scans are milliseconds, and the largest table (Message) is already indexed. It only becomes a multi-second lock-holding operation at the 10^5–10^6-row child-table scale the autovacuum tuning migration anticipates, and even then it's a transient stall on hot inbox tables, not a correctness or security flaw. The proposed fix (one additive migration mirroring 20260602120000) is cheap and correct.

### 8. Conversation_lastMessagePreview_trgm_idx is maintained on every message send/receive but no query plan can use it (cross-table OR); in-file comment doesn't know it exists

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `prisma/schema.prisma:854-855` · **Area:** db-queries-indexes

**Root cause:** The trgm GIN on Conversation.lastMessagePreview ('pg_trgm GIN for the inbox-list search bar's ILIKE on lastMessagePreview') has exactly one consumer: the inbox-list search clause in lib/queries/conversations.ts:104-112, where `lastMessagePreview contains` is OR'd with two Contact RELATION filters (`contact.name contains`, `contact.phoneNumber contains`). Prisma renders relation filters as a join/EXISTS, and Postgres cannot BitmapOr across tables, so no plan shape can ever use this GIN for that query — the planner walks the team's conversations and filters. That is fine (and the code says so), but the index is dead weight: lastMessagePreview is rewritten by the conversation summary bump on es…

**Impact:** Pure write amplification on the single hottest UPDATE in the system (the conversation summary bump runs once per message, inbound and outbound), plus misleading documentation that will cause someone to 'add' an index that already exists. No user-visible latency issue — the search itself is bounded by the team's conversation count, as the code documents.

**Recommended fix:** Drop Conversation_lastMessagePreview_trgm_idx (one-line migration) and fix the conversations.ts comment to state the real plan (team-partition walk, trgm impossible while the OR spans the Contact join). If list search ever shows up hot, restructure into two indexed branches (contact-id prefilter + preview-only query, unioned) — only then re-add the GIN.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced every element of the claim. (1) The trgm GIN index exists (schema.prisma:854-855, created in 0_init/migration.sql:853). (2) Repo-wide grep confirms the ONLY where-clause consumer of Conversation.lastMessagePreview is apps/api/src/lib/queries/conversations.ts:104-112, where the preview ILIKE is OR'd with two Contact RELATION filters; team-chat hits are the unrelated TeamChannel.lastMessagePreview column and no raw SQL touches it. (3) Postgres cannot BitmapOr across relations — whether Prisma renders the relation filters as EXISTS/IN subqueries or a join, the OR becomes a post-scan filter and the GIN can never be the access path, so the index is plan-dead. (4) The indexed column is rewritten on every inbound (ingest.ts:642-652) and outbound (commit-outbound-send.ts:52) message — non-HOT updates with GIN maintenance on the system's hottest UPDATE. (5) The comment at conversations.ts:54-56 explicitly claims no trgm index exists and suggests adding one past ~50k conversations, contradicting the schema. Not by_design: the 2026-06-01 index-cleanup migration dropped other redundant indexes for exactly this 'pure write-overhead removal' rationale but missed this one, so removal matches project policy rather than violating a locked decision. Severity stays low: pure write amplification + disk + misleading docs, no user-visible latency or correctness impact at pilot scale.

### 9. Redundant btree indexes on the hottest-write tables (Message ×2, InternalNote, Contact) survived the 2026-06-01 'pure write-overhead removal' cleanup

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `prisma/schema.prisma:939-940, 1282, 672` · **Area:** db-queries-indexes

**Root cause:** Four bare/duplicated indexes are fully covered by existing composites: (1) Message `@@index([teamId])` (line 940) is a leftmost prefix of both the dedupe unique `(teamId, channel, externalId)` (938) and `Message_teamId_timestamp_id_idx` (969). (2) Message `@@index([conversationId, timestamp])` (939) is fully covered by `(conversationId, timestamp DESC, id DESC)` (951): Postgres scans btrees in both directions, so the DESC,DESC index serves equality-on-conversationId + timestamp range/ASC ordering too — the justifying comment at 947-950 ('The ascending index above still serves chronological/range scans; this one is purpose-built for the backward keyset cursor') has it backwards: the purpose-b…

**Impact:** Two unnecessary index maintenances per Message insert (highest-write table; every webhook inbound, agent send, broadcast recipient row) plus one per InternalNote and Contact write; larger index footprint = more autovacuum and cache pressure on the 8GB VPS. No correctness or latency bug.

**Recommended fix:** One migration: DROP INDEX Message_teamId_idx, Message_conversationId_timestamp_idx, InternalNote_teamId_idx, Contact_teamId_idx (confirm idx_scan≈0 attribution via pg_stat_user_indexes first — any scans they show are absorbed by the covering composites), and remove the four `@@index` lines + fix the schema comment at 947-950.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced every element of the claim from the code. (1) Message `@@index([teamId])` (schema.prisma:940) is a leftmost prefix of both `@@unique([teamId, channel, externalId])` (:938) and `Message_teamId_timestamp_id_idx` (:969) — any teamId-equality lookup, including the Team→Message FK-cascade scan, rides those. (2) Message `@@index([conversationId, timestamp])` (:939) is fully covered by `@@index([conversationId, timestamp DESC, id DESC])` (:951): Postgres btrees scan in both directions, and every ASC-ordered call site has equality on conversationId (apps/api/src/lib/queries/search.ts:161 and conversations.ts:560, both `WHERE conversationId = $1 … ORDER BY [timestamp asc, id asc]`), which a backward scan of the DESC,DESC index satisfies exactly — and BETTER than the 2-col ASC index, which cannot even serve the `id ASC` tiebreaker. No query in the repo orders across multiple conversations by (conversationId ASC, timestamp ASC), the only shape the DESC index couldn't serve. The justifying comment at schema.prisma:947-950 ('The ascending index above still serves chronological/range scans') is indeed inverted, as the finding says. (3) InternalNote `@@index([teamId])` (:1282) is a prefix of `InternalNote_teamId_timestamp_id_idx` (:1292). (4) Contact `@@index([teamId])` (:672) is a prefix of five composites (:667, :673, :674, :677, :679). All four indexes physically exist (0_init/migration.sql:837, 867, 883, 889) and no later migration drops them. Not by_design: the project's own 2026-06-01 cleanup (20260601130000_contact_email_trgm_and_index_cleanup/migration.sql:15-17, 29-30) dropped the identical pattern on Tag/AudienceGroup as 'Pure write-overhead removal', and docs/production-audit-2026-06-01.md:1027-1034 shows that audit only examined Tag/AudienceGroup — no doc records a deliberate decision to keep these four. Severity LOW is correct (and matches the reporter): impact is two extra index maintenances per insert on the hottest-write table (every inbound webhook, agent send, broadcast recipient) plus one per InternalNote/Contact write and extra index footprint/autovacuum pressure on the 8GB VPS — real but bounded, no correctness or user-visible latency bug at pilot scale. The proposed fix (one DROP INDEX migration after a pg_stat_user_indexes sanity check, plus removing the @@index lines and correcting the :947-950 comment) is sound; only caveat is that leftmost-prefix scans on the wider composites are marginally fatter per entry than the dedicated btrees, a tradeoff the project already accepted in the Tag/AudienceGroup precedent.

### 10. /contacts list pays O(all live contacts in team) per page — lateral probe + un-indexable COALESCE sort runs in full for every page, regardless of filters

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `apps/api/src/lib/queries/contacts.ts:96-183` · **Area:** db-queries-indexes

**Root cause:** listContacts sorts by `COALESCE(conv."lastMessageAt", c."createdAt") DESC, c.id DESC` (line 181) where `conv` comes from a LEFT JOIN LATERAL (96-109). Because the sort key is an expression spanning the lateral join, no index can serve the ORDER BY, and the keyset cursor predicate (171-179) references the same expression — so for EVERY page request (including page 2, 3 … of infinite scroll) Postgres must execute the lateral probe (one Conversation_teamId_contactId_key seek) for every contact passing `teamId + deletedAt IS NULL`, materialize N sort keys, top-N sort, then LIMIT 51. The file meticulously documents the customFields-ILIKE seq-scan as a 'KNOWN INDEX-MISS (acceptable at pilot scale)…

**Impact:** Pilot-bounded (a few thousand contacts → single-digit ms, matching the file's own scale posture), but at the 50k-contact trigger the file already names for the search branch, every contacts-page render and every scroll-page becomes ~50k unique-index probes plus a 50k-row sort (~hundreds of ms), multiplied across agents. It will surface before the documented search miss does, because it doesn't req…

**Recommended fix:** No action at pilot scale beyond documenting it next to the existing KNOWN INDEX-MISS note with the same 50k trigger. When it trips: denormalize the sort key onto Contact (e.g. `lastActivityAt` bumped by the same ingest/send paths that maintain lastInboundAt and the conversation summary), index `(teamId, lastActivityAt DESC, id DESC)`, and key both ORDER BY and the cursor off the column — turning every page into a pure keyset index walk.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced from the code. In apps/api/src/lib/queries/contacts.ts, listContacts orders by `COALESCE(conv."lastMessageAt", c."createdAt") DESC, c.id DESC` (line 181) where `conv` is the output of a LEFT JOIN LATERAL into Conversation (lines 96-109). Because the sort key is an expression over the lateral join's output, no Contact index can produce the order, and the keyset cursor predicate (lines 170-179) references the same COALESCE expression, so it can only be evaluated AFTER the join — it never prunes the underlying Contact scan. Consequently every page request (page 1 and every infinite-scroll page alike) must: (1) scan all live contacts in the team via the partial Contact_teamId_active_idx, (2) run the lateral probe (one Conversation_teamId_contactId_key unique-index seek, schema.prisma:853) per contact, (3) evaluate the COALESCE sort key for all of them, (4) top-N heapsort, LIMIT 51. The auditor's supporting claims also check out: Contact has no denormalized last-activity column — only lastInboundAt (schema.prisma, comment explicitly says "most recent inbound message timestamp"), which excludes outbound sends and so cannot replace the sort key as-is; a repo-wide grep for lastActivityAt returns nothing. This is NOT covered by any prior audit or documented tradeoff: the 2026-05-30 L15 finding and the in-file KNOWN INDEX-MISS comment (contacts.ts:122-134) cover only the search-branch customFields::text seq-scan; the 2026-06-01 MEDIUM LATERAL finding was the contactId-only probe, since fixed by adding `co."teamId" = c."teamId"` (contacts.ts:104-105 with explanatory comment); the email trgm miss was fixed with Contact_email_trgm_idx. CLAUDE.md's "Scaling cliffs to anticipate" list does not include this. So it is a real, undocumented latent scaling characteristic — not by_design (nothing documents it) and not refuted (the mechanics are exactly as described). Severity LOW is correct: zero impact at pilot scale (a few thousand contacts = single-digit-ms; each lateral probe is a warm unique-index seek), it is bounded per-tenant by the partial index, and it only becomes user-visible (~hundreds of ms per contacts-page render/scroll, multiplied across agents) at the ~50k-contacts-per-team scale the file itself names as the revisit trigger for the adjacent search miss. The proposed fix (document now; denormalize a lastActivityAt onto Contact + index (teamId, lastActivityAt DESC, id DESC) when the trigger trips) is sound and matches the project's don't-pre-build posture. One minor overstatement in the title: "regardless of filters" — search/stage/source/tag filters on `c` ARE applied before/during the Contact scan and shrink the probed row count; what is truly filter-independent is that the CURSOR never prunes, so deeper pages cost the same as page 1.

### 11. Per-socket rate-limit 'silent drop' actually raises an uncaughtException per dropped frame (no socket 'error' listener)

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `apps/api/src/realtime/realtime.gateway.ts:199-214` · **Area:** realtime-server

**Root cause:** The per-socket emit cap rejects over-budget frames with `next(new Error("rate_limited"))`, with a comment claiming 'Pure drop is the right behavior'. It is not a pure drop: in socket.io 4.8.3, a socket-middleware error routes through `dispatch()` → `process.nextTick` → `_onerror(err)` → `this.emitReserved("error", err)` (node_modules socket.io/dist/socket.js:689-695, 520-526). Neither the gateway nor @nestjs/platform-socket.io registers a `socket.on("error")` listener (verified by grep — zero hits in apps/api/src and the NestJS websockets package), so Node's EventEmitter 'error'-event special case throws ERR_UNHANDLED_ERROR inside the nextTick, which lands in the process-level `uncaughtException` handler.

**Impact:** A hostile or runaway client that exceeds 240 events/10s converts every subsequent frame into a synchronous throw + full-stack console.error (synchronous stdout write) instead of a quiet drop — sustained log spam and avoidable event-loop work during exactly the abuse scenario the bucket exists to absorb. No crash (process handler catches), no correctness impact.

**Recommended fix:** Register a no-op `client.on("error", () => {})` in handleConnection (optionally logging at debug with sampling), or implement the drop without an Error by tracking the bucket inside a wrapper that simply doesn't call next() — er, socket.io requires next, so the listener is the clean fix. One line.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently re-derived the full chain and reproduced the core Node behavior. The per-socket rate-limit middleware at realtime.gateway.ts:199-214 rejects over-budget frames with next(new Error("rate_limited")) under a comment claiming a silent/pure drop. In the installed socket.io@4.8.3, a socket-middleware error routes dispatch() -> process.nextTick -> _onerror(err) -> emitReserved("error", err), and StrictEventEmitter extends Node's events.EventEmitter, so emitReserved is a plain emit("error"). No "error" listener exists on connection sockets anywhere: not in apps/api/src (grep hits are only ioredis/BullMQ/ffmpeg/safe-fetch), not in ws-adapter.ts, not in @nestjs/platform-socket.io or @nestjs/websockets@11.1.21 (ERROR_EVENT constant is exported but never used), and there is no @SubscribeMessage("error"). Node's EventEmitter error special-case therefore throws inside the nextTick; I reproduced that this lands in a process-level uncaughtException handler and the process survives — main.ts:51-53 has exactly that handler (console.error and return), so each dropped frame becomes a full [uncaughtException] stack-trace log line instead of a quiet drop. The wire-side intent holds (_onerror emits locally only; nothing is sent back to the client, so no reply-amplification), but server-side the drop is not silent. Impact is real but bounded to the abuse scenario the bucket exists for: sustained log spam + per-frame exception/stack-capture overhead while a hostile or runaway client exceeds 240 events/10s, plus the risk of masking genuine uncaughtExceptions in the noise. No crash, no correctness impact, unreachable in legitimate use (typing throttles client-side at 3/s vs the ~24/s sustained cap). Severity 'low' as reported is correct (hardening/log hygiene, not a pilot-visible bug); the one-line fix (no-op client.on("error") in handleConnection, optionally sampled debug logging) is sound. Not by_design: the in-code comment shows the author believed it was a pure drop — the uncaughtException path is unintended, not a documented tradeoff.

### 12. typing:thread:start accepts an arbitrary threadRootId — non-member's userId can be injected into a private channel thread's typing indicator

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `apps/api/src/realtime/realtime.gateway.ts:798-822` · **Area:** realtime-server

**Root cause:** The thread-typing handler gates only on `client.rooms.has(channelRoom(body.channelId))` — membership in the CLIENT-SUPPLIED channelId — then writes `this.typing.addThread(body.threadRootId, userId, client.id)` without verifying that threadRootId is a message in that channel (the doc-comment even says 'The thread root's parent channel is the only thing we trust the client about; everything else is rederived server-side' — but threadRootId is also trusted). A team member who belongs to any channel (the default channel auto-passes membership, gateway:694) can pass their own channelId plus the threadRootId of a thread in a PRIVATE channel they were excluded from. The direct emit goes harmlessly to their own channel room, but the typing registry entry is keyed by threadRootId alone.

**Impact:** Same-team integrity spoofing only: a member excluded from a private channel can make themselves appear to be typing in its threads (social-engineering / confusion value; no read access gained, no cross-team name resolution since foreign userIds won't resolve in the victim's member catalog). Persists until the attacker's socket disconnects.

**Recommended fix:** On first registration of a (channelId, threadRootId) pair per socket, verify the thread root message belongs to body.channelId (`teamChannelMessage.findFirst({ id: threadRootId, channelId, teamId })`) — mirrors the subscribe:channel first-join pattern with the same budget; re-toggles of an already-validated composite skip the lookup.

> Verifier (high confidence): The finding accurately describes the code. The `typing:thread:start` handler (apps/api/src/realtime/realtime.gateway.ts:798-822) is fully synchronous and gates authorization ONLY on `client.rooms.has(channelRoom(body.channelId))` (line 808) — membership in the client-supplied channelId — then calls `this.typing.addThread(body.threadRootId, ...)` (line 812) with NO validation that threadRootId belongs to that channel. This contrasts with the sibling `subscribe:channel` handler (lines 663-712) which does a real DB membership check (teamChannel.findFirst + teamChannelMember.findUnique). TypingService.thread (typing.service.ts:13) is a process-global map keyed solely by threadRootId. A team member in any channel (the default channel auto-passes membership) can pass channelId=their-own-channel + threadRootId=a thread root in a private channel they were excluded from, injecting their userId into snapshotThread(X). The injected userId does NOT reach channel-B members directly (the attacker's emit targets the attacker's own channel room), but surfaces when a legitimate channel-B member next types in thread X — their emit carries snapshotThread(X) to channelRoom(B), now including the attacker, and the client (typing-indicator.tsx:21 + thread-panel.tsx:51-55 namesById built from the team-wide teamMembers catalog) renders the attacker's real name as 'is typing…'. Impact is same-team integrity/spoofing only: no read access to the private channel's content is gained, and it requires knowing an opaque server-generated message ID from a channel the attacker can't read (IDs aren't exposed to non-members), so exploitability is narrow. Confirms the finding's own 'low' rating — a real authorization gap but bounded, cosmetic, and precondition-heavy. Not critical/high: no cross-tenant leak, no auth bypass, no data loss, no content disclosure.

### 13. buildVisibleOnlineSnapshot has no error handling on the connect/disconnect paths — DB flap during a reconnect storm produces unhandled rejections and silently lost presence frames

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `apps/api/src/realtime/realtime.gateway.ts:264-275, 450-455, 232, 244` · **Area:** realtime-server

**Root cause:** `buildVisibleOnlineSnapshot` calls `this.db.user.findMany` with no try/catch (gateway:264-275) — deliberately unlike its sibling `buildVisibleViewers`, which wraps the same read and falls back to the unfiltered set 'under transient Postgres flapping' (gateway:294-308). Three call sites leak the rejection: (1) handleDisconnect:450 `void this.buildVisibleOnlineSnapshot(teamId).then(...)` — a `.then` with no `.catch`, so a DB error becomes an unhandledRejection AND the team never receives the went-offline presence:update; (2) handleConnection:232 `await this.buildVisibleOnlineSnapshot(...)` — a throw rejects handleConnection itself, which NestJS does not catch for gateway lifecycle hooks; (3) the availability snapshot path at :244.

**Impact:** During a Postgres flap a user's last-socket disconnect can leave them showing green to the whole team until the next presence change ticks (self-healing, since any later connect/disconnect/status flip rebuilds the snapshot), connecting sockets can paint with empty presence until the client's `presence:request` reseed, and the logs fill with raw [unhandledRejection] entries lacking the gateway context.

**Recommended fix:** Wrap the findMany in buildVisibleOnlineSnapshot in try/catch and fall back to the unfiltered `connected` list (the exact posture buildVisibleViewers documents — over-showing beats going blank), and/or add `.catch(err => this.logger.error(...))` at the three call sites. Same for emitAvailabilitySnapshot.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced every mechanical claim. buildVisibleOnlineSnapshot (apps/api/src/realtime/realtime.gateway.ts:264-275) performs a bare db.user.findMany with no try/catch, while its sibling buildVisibleViewers (lines 294-308) wraps the identical read and documents the fallback posture ('over-showing beats going blank under transient Postgres flapping', lines 284-286) — proving the gap is an omission, not a deliberate posture, and no locked decision covers it. Three rejection-leaking call paths confirmed: (1) handleDisconnect line 450-455 uses `void ...then(...)` with no .catch, so a DB error during a last-socket disconnect drops the team-wide went-offline presence:update and floats the rejection; (2) handleConnection line 232 awaits it bare, and the framework discards the hook's promise (verified in apps/api/node_modules/@nestjs/websockets/web-sockets-controller.js:78 — `.subscribe((args) => instance.handleConnection(...args))`), so a throw also skips the connecting socket's presence seed + availability snapshot (lines 235-254); (3) fanout-rules.ts:464 fires emitter.emitPresenceSnapshot as a floating promise, which awaits the same helper (emitter.service.ts:103, bound at gateway:66-68) with no catch. emitAvailabilitySnapshot (gateway:337-364) shares the gap, void-called at gateway:244. Impact is real but tightly bounded: main.ts:54-56's process-level unhandledRejection handler prevents a crash, presence frames carry the full onlineUserIds list so the next successful presence event fully converges (self-healing), and the client re-fires presence:request on connect as a reseed. Worst case is a user showing green after going offline until the next presence tick, a fresh tab painting grey/empty presence until reseed, and uncorrelated [unhandledRejection] log noise during a DB flap. No data loss, no security impact, no customer-message impact — 'low' is the correct severity, matching the reporter's own rating.

### 14. Server re-seed clobbers a filtered conversation list back to the pruned unfiltered head page on router.refresh

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `apps/web/src/features/team-chat/hooks/use-team-events.ts:199-210` · **Area:** realtime-client-state

**Root cause:** The re-seed effect replaces `conversations` with `initialConversations.filter(rowMatchesFilterFor(filterRef.current, ...))` whenever the seed key (team + id set + cursor) changes. initialConversations is always the UNFILTERED 25-row head (page.tsx calls listConversations() with no filter), while a non-default filter view (Mine/Unassigned/Closed/stage) was loaded server-side-filtered via the filter-change refetch. A router.refresh() — fired on every successful contact-panel field save (contact-panel.tsx:696 `startSaving(() => router.refresh())`) — re-runs page.tsx; if any message arrived since mount the head id-set changed, the re-seed fires, and the filtered list collapses to the unfiltered top-25's filtered subset.

**Impact:** While on a Closed/Mine/stage view, editing a contact field can visibly truncate the list to the few filtered rows present in the team-wide top-25, with the rest demoted behind Load-more. Pagination stays consistent (any filtered row newer than the cursor is in the kept head), and the next resync (visibility, reconnect, any non-optimistic teammate frame on a non-broad view) restores the full page.

**Recommended fix:** When `filterRef.current` is a non-default filter, skip adopting the unfiltered seed wholesale: either ignore the re-seed and schedule a filter-aware resyncOnce(), or merge the seed rows into the existing list instead of replacing it.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced the full chain from code. (1) The SSR seed is deliberately unfiltered: apps/web/src/app/(app)/inbox/page.tsx:72 calls listConversations() with no filter, and apps/web/src/lib/api/queries.ts:215-221 documents "Deliberately UNFILTERED" (the sub-sidebar's closed-count fallback needs closed rows in the seed). (2) The active filter is client state that survives router.refresh(): InboxFilterProvider holds useState<Filter> (inbox-filter-context.tsx), and inbox-shell.tsx:599-607 passes it into useTeamEvents, where filterRef mirrors it. (3) On a non-default view (Mine/Unassigned/Closed/stage) the list was replaced by the filter-change refetch (use-team-events.ts:284-344) with the FULL filtered first page from /api/conversations?filter=... (4) The re-seed effect (use-team-events.ts:199-210) compares an order-sensitive key `teamId|ids.join(",")|cursor`; whenever router.refresh() delivers a head whose id order/membership/cursor changed (true any time a message arrived anywhere on the team since the last seed, since the head sorts by recency), it REPLACES conversations with initialConversations.filter(rowMatchesFilterFor(filterRef.current,...)) — i.e. the unfiltered top-30 intersected with the filter, which can be 0-few rows — and resets nextCursor to the unfiltered cursor. (5) The trigger is real: contact-panel.tsx:696 (field save) and :1306 (tags save) fire startSaving(() => router.refresh()) on every successful save. Not by_design: the re-seed comment (lines 190-198) only documents pruning closed rows for the default view and avoiding blow-away on identical data; it never acknowledges clobbering a narrow filtered view, and the filter-change refetch exists precisely to load the full matching set those views need. Severity stays LOW: the finding's own bounding holds — keyset cursor on (lastMessageAt,id) (apps/api/src/lib/queries/conversations.ts:92-96) means loadMore(filter + unfiltered cursor) leaves no pagination gap (every filtered row newer than the cursor is in the kept head); recovery is automatic via the visibility resync (use-team-events.ts:1012-1021), reconnect resync, or any non-optimistic teammate frame (scheduleFilterResync only no-ops on active/all, lines 520-522, so it DOES fire on the affected narrow views); and the manifestation requires the conjunction of a non-default filter + a contact-panel save + head drift since the last seed. Transient visible truncation behind a still-working Load-more, no data loss, no security impact.

### 15. List refetch chains lack rejection handling — network failure during a filter switch or load-older surfaces unhandled promise rejections and leaves a partial list with no retry

**Severity:** low · **Location:** `apps/web/src/features/team-chat/hooks/use-team-events.ts:307-340` · **Area:** realtime-client-state

**Root cause:** The filter-change refetch is `fetchWithSessionGuard(...).then(...).then(...)` with no .catch — a network-level rejection is unhandled, and because the instant client-side re-derive already pruned the list (lines 303-305), the view stays a partial local slice (only rows that happened to be loaded) with nothing scheduled to retry until an unrelated resync. loadMore (lines 256-268) has .finally but no .catch — same unhandled rejection, button just silently does nothing. In use-conversation-events.ts, loadOlder (lines 551-606) wraps the fetch in try/finally without catch, so a fetch rejection propagates to `void loadOlder(...)` callers as an unhandled rejection mid-scroll. Contrast: resyncOnce and the other recovery paths follow a silent-degrade convention.

**Impact:** On a flaky connection, switching to Mine/Closed/stage shows an incomplete list that never self-corrects until a later resync trigger, plus console unhandled-rejection noise that pollutes real error triage. Bounded — every path eventually converges via the reconnect/visibility resync.

**Recommended fix:** Add .catch(() => {}) (or a retry-once) to the filter-change chain and loadMore, and a catch in loadOlder that returns 0 — matching the silent-degrade convention the sibling recovery paths already follow.

> Verifier (high confidence): Independently reproduced all three claims from the code. (1) The filter-change refetch chain in use-team-events.ts has no .catch (chain ends at line 340), and the instant client-side prune at lines 303-305 runs synchronously before the fetch — so a rejected fetch leaves a partial local slice with no retry scheduled (the effect only re-runs on filterKey change). (2) loadMore (lines 250-269) is .then().then().finally() with no catch — rejection is unhandled, button silently no-ops. (3) loadOlder in use-conversation-events.ts (551-606) is try/finally with no catch, and all callers (message-thread.tsx:657/690 `void loadOlder().then(...)`, use-chat-scroll.ts:384-429 `.finally()` only) propagate the rejection unhandled; the IntersectionObserver pagination won't re-fire until the sentinel exits/re-enters the viewport, so scroll-up history loading can stall. Crucially, rejections are a DESIGNED code path, not theoretical: client-session-guard.ts:113 deliberately throws "transient upstream 401" during NestJS restarts with the comment "Throw so callers' existing error paths fire" — these callers have none. The finding's contrast claim also holds: sibling recovery paths (resyncOnce catch at use-team-events.ts:451-453, runBackfill .catch at use-conversation-events.ts:831, runFullRefetch .catch at :948) all follow the silent-degrade convention, so the missing catches are oversights against the file's own convention, not a locked decision.
